Motivation: To rigorously understand time-dependent control of Majorana modes in topological platforms, distinguishing operational benchmarks from microscopic boundary effects, particularly how wave-packet truncation manifests in quantum field theory beyond naive spatial cutting.

Method: Models truncation as a time-dependent chiral rotation in a 1+1D massless Majorana field; analyzes asymptotic (Bogoliubov) and local (causal filter) limits; derives infrared soft-mode spectrum β(ω,ν) ∝ (ω+ν)⁻¹; computes Hilbert–Schmidt norm divergence; proves exact local field identities for even observables; establishes equivalence of retained sector to single-particle state and discarded sector to vacuum in pair-production-free regime.

Result: Two complementary limits: (i) Global limit yields universal infrared memory with β(ω,ν) ∝ (ω+ν)⁻¹ and logarithmic orthogonality catastrophe; persists under smooth switching in massless limit. (ii) Local limit (no pair production) yields exact causal truncation: retained sector ≅ single-particle state, discarded sector ≅ vacuum within even local observable algebra.

Conclusion: Global infrared memory and local causal truncation are dual diagnostics of the same dynamical operation; provides rigorous field-theoretic foundation for time-resolved control in topological quantum systems, decoupling effective operation from boundary details.

Abstract: We investigate the quantum field dynamics of truncating a Majorana wave packet via a time-dependent channel shutter. Modeling truncation as a controlled modification of chiral propagation rather than a literal spatial cut, we analyze a $1+1$D massless Majorana field subjected to a time-dependent rotation between its chiral components. We demonstrate that this protocol manifests two complementary physical limits. Globally, a mismatch between early- and late-time channel identifications induces a fermionic Bogoliubov transformation. We prove this asymptotic change universally generates an infrared soft-mode memory, $β(ω, ν) \propto (ω+ν)^{-1}$, leading to a logarithmic divergence in the Hilbert--Schmidt norm. This orthogonality-catastrophe-like obstruction persists even under infinitely smooth switching in the massless limit. Conversely, in the number-conserving regime where pair production vanishes, the shutter acts as a purely causal filter. We establish exact local field identities showing that, restricted to the even local observable algebra, the retained sector is exactly equivalent to a single-particle state while the discarded sector reduces to the vacuum. Ultimately, we show that global infrared memory and local causal truncation are complementary diagnostics of the same dynamical operation. These results provide a rigorous field-theoretic foundation for time-dependent control in topological platforms, cleanly separating effective operational benchmarks from microscopic boundary dynamics.

</details>


### [127] [Anomalies and discrete torsion in Type II and Type I worldsheet theories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30774)
*Masashi Kawahira,Shotaro Kawanago,Shota Saito,Hiroki Wada*

Main category: hep-th

TL;DR: This paper analyzes anomalies and discrete torsion in Type II and Type I string worldsheet theories on general 10D target spaces, using the Anderson dual of bordism theory (Yamashita–Yonekura model) to show anomaly cancellation requires orientability and spin structure; it also classifies discrete torsion distinguishing IIA/IIB and standard/Sugimoto Type I.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously characterize and cancel anomalies in Type II and Type I string worldsheet theories on arbitrary ten-dimensional target spaces, leveraging modern topological field theory tools—specifically invertible field theories classified by the Anderson dual of bordism—and to clarify the physical role and classification of discrete torsion.

Method: Applies the explicit model of the Anderson dual of bordism theory (developed by Yamashita and Yonekura) to compute the anomaly as a 3D invertible field theory; uses bordism-theoretic classification to derive necessary and sufficient conditions for anomaly cancellation; analyzes discrete torsion via cohomological data tied to spacetime structure and orientifold projections.

Result: Anomaly cancellation occurs iff the target space is orientable and admits a spin structure—unifying constraints for both Type II and Type I; discrete torsion is fully determined and physically interpreted: it encodes the choice between Type IIA/IIB (via H^3(X;ℤ)) and between ordinary Type I and Sugimoto models (via orientifold action on B-field and fermionic boundary conditions).

Conclusion: The topological consistency of Type II and Type I strings on a 10D manifold is governed solely by orientability and spin structure—no further characteristic-class obstructions arise; discrete torsion is not an independent parameter but a derived consequence of spacetime topology and the specific orientifold/parity action defining the theory.

Abstract: We provide a comprehensive study of anomalies and discrete torsion in the worldsheet theories of Type II and Type I string theories on a general ten-dimensional target space. In the modern understanding, the anomaly of a worldsheet theory is characterized by an invertible field theory in three dimensions, and invertible field theories are classified by the Anderson dual of a suitable bordism theory. To study the anomaly cancellation condition, we employ the explicit model of the Anderson dual constructed by Yamashita and Yonekura. We find that, for both Type II and Type I string theories, the anomaly of the worldsheet theory is cancelled if and only if the target space is orientable and admits a spin structure. We also determine the discrete torsion and clarify its physical meaning. It includes the distinctions between Type IIA and Type IIB string theories and between ordinary Type I and Sugimoto string theories.

</details>


### [128] [Holographic subregion complexity in insulator/superconductor transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30852)
*Yu Shi,Chikun Ding,Yuebing Zhou,Weike Deng,Sheng Long*

Main category: hep-th

TL;DR: This paper investigates holographic subregion complexity (HSC) across a backreacted insulator/superconductor transition in an AdS-soliton background, comparing it with HEE and CV-complexity; HSC detects the second-order transition and exhibits distinct qualitative behavior—such as discontinuous jumps and non-monotonic dependence on chemical potential—depending on subsystem size and entanglement-wedge topology, while matching CV complexity analytically for half-space after normalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether and how holographic subregion complexity (HSC) can diagnose quantum phase transitions—specifically the insulator/superconductor transition—and to compare its diagnostic power and qualitative behavior with established probes like holographic entanglement entropy (HEE) and complexity=volume (CV).

Method: The authors perform fully backreacted gravitational calculations in an AdS-soliton geometry dual to an insulator/superconductor system; they compute HSC (using the 'complexity=action' or analogous subregion prescription), HEE via Ryu-Takayanagi surfaces, and CV-complexity for strip and half-space subsystems; they analyze phase structure, critical behavior, and functional dependencies (e.g., on strip width ℓ and chemical potential μ), including normalization against insulating references.

Result: HSC signals the second-order insulator/superconductor transition; for strip subsystems, it shows a finite jump at critical width ℓ_c (unlike continuous HEE); HSC grows linearly with ℓ beyond ℓ_c while HEE saturates; HSC is non-monotonic in μ for ℓ < ℓ_c but monotonic for ℓ > ℓ_c; after normalization/subtraction, half-space HSC and CV complexity densities are analytically identical.

Conclusion: HSC is a sensitive probe of the insulator/superconductor transition, but its qualitative response—discontinuities, monotonicity, scaling—is strongly dependent on subsystem geometry and entanglement-wedge topology (e.g., connected vs. disconnected RT surfaces), distinguishing it from HEE and aligning it analytically with CV only in the half-space limit.

Abstract: We study holographic subregion complexity (HSC) across a fully backreacted insulator/superconductor transition in an AdS-soliton background and compare it with holographic entanglement entropy (HEE) and holographic complexity based on the complexity=volume (CV) proposal. Both HSC and HEE signal the second-order transition. For a strip subsystem, competing connected and disconnected Ryu-Takayanagi surfaces give rise to a confinement/deconfinement transition. At fixed chemical potential in the superconducting phase, HSC exhibits a finite jump at the critical width, whereas HEE remains continuous. Beyond this width, HSC grows linearly with the strip width, while HEE is constant. At fixed strip width, HSC first decreases and then increases with chemical potential for $\ell<\ell_c$, opposite to HEE, but increases monotonically for $\ell>\ell_c$. After consistent normalization and subtraction of the respective insulating references, the half-space HSC and CV complexity densities are analytically identical. These results show that HSC can diagnose the insulator/superconductor transition, but its qualitative response remains sensitive to the subsystem scale and entanglement-wedge topology.

</details>


### [129] [The large $N$ vector model with angular velocity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31151)
*Justin R. David,Srijan Kumar*

Main category: hep-th

TL;DR: This paper computes the large-N free energy of the critical O(N) vector model on S¹×S² with angular velocity μ̂, finding a pole at μ̂²r²=1 in the leading high-temperature term, whose residue matches the massless free theory; sub-leading terms are non-analytic in μ̂ and distinguish the critical fixed point from the free theory; consistency is confirmed via pp-wave geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand thermal properties and universality of the critical O(N) vector model at large N in a rotating background (S¹×S²), particularly how angular velocity affects the free energy and whether singularities reflect underlying effective field theory expectations or distinguish critical vs. free behavior.

Method: Analytic large-N expansion of the free energy on S¹×S² with angular velocity μ̂, performed near μ̂r = 0 and near the light-cone-like singularity μ̂²r² = 1; supplemented by numerical evaluation to bridge regimes; cross-checked using mapping to pp-wave geometry.

Result: Leading high-temperature free energy exhibits a pole at μ̂²r² = 1 with residue matching the massless free theory; sub-leading terms show non-analytic (e.g., square-root) dependence on (1 − μ̂²r²), distinguishing the critical fixed point from the free theory; pp-wave computation independently reproduces the same pole residue.

Conclusion: The pole structure and its universal residue confirm predictions of thermal effective field theory, while non-analytic sub-leading terms encode genuine criticality beyond free-field behavior—demonstrating how rotation probes conformal symmetry breaking and fixed-point uniqueness.

Abstract: We study the free energy of the critical $O(N)$ vector model at large $N$ on $S^{1}\times S^{2}$ with an angular velocity $\hatμ$ without the singlet constraint. The leading high-temperature behaviour is determined analytically both as an expansion about $\hatμr=0$ and $\hatμ^{2}r^{2}=1$ where $r$ is the radius of the sphere. We supplement the analytic results with a numerical analysis that agrees with both the analytical expansions in their respective regimes and smoothly interpolates between them. The leading high-temperature contribution to the free energy develops a pole at $\hatμ^{2}r^{2}=1$, in agreement with expectations from the thermal effective field theory. Its residue coincides with that of the massless free theory. Sub-leading terms, however, exhibit non-analytic dependence on the angular velocity and distinguish the critical fixed-point result from the free theory answer. The residue at the pole can also be obtained by placing the $O(N)$ model on the pp-wave geometry. We show that the residue agrees with that obtained from the direct computation.

</details>


### [130] [Coarse-grained models for loop quantum gravity and their renormalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31152)
*Mehdi Assanioussi,Martin Zeiß*

Main category: hep-th

TL;DR: This paper introduces a family of effective theories for canonical loop quantum gravity, built via systematic coarse-graining of spin network states, yielding parameterized coarse Hilbert spaces and effective Hamiltonians; it further formulates a non-perturbative renormalization framework with explicit flow equations to probe the continuum limit and phenomenological model derivation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a framework for studying the emergence of the continuum limit and deriving phenomenological models from canonical loop quantum gravity.

Method: Systematic coarse-graining of spin network states to construct coarse Hilbert spaces; derivation of effective Hamiltonians by analyzing how coarse-graining interacts with LQG Hamiltonian operators; implementation of a non-perturbative renormalization framework with explicit renormalization flow equations.

Result: A family of parameterized effective theories, each comprising a coarse Hilbert space and an effective Hamiltonian; a well-defined non-perturbative renormalization prescription with explicit flow equations for coarse-grained models.

Conclusion: The proposed coarse-grained effective theories and their renormalization framework provide a concrete arena to investigate continuum emergence and phenomenology in canonical loop quantum gravity.

Abstract: We introduce a family of effective theories and use them to define a specific type of coarse-grained models for canonical loop quantum gravity. Each effective theory is characterized by two parameters and it consists mainly of a Hilbert space of coarse states and an effective Hamiltonian operator. The construction of the coarse Hilbert spaces relies on a systematic coarse-graining procedure for spin network states. The effective Hamiltonians are then obtained from an analysis of the interplay between this coarse-graining procedure and the action of various Hamiltonian operators defined in loop quantum gravity. Finally, we provide a prescription for implementing the Hamiltonian non-perturbative renormalization framework for the coarse-grained models, and we explicitly write down the renormalization flow equations. The aim of this work is to build an arena for studying the emergence of the continuum limit and the derivation of phenomenological models in the context of canonical loop quantum gravity.

</details>


### [131] [Superfluid memory effect](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.31172)
*Kristan Jensen,Alfredo Pérez,Stefan Prohazka*

Main category: hep-th

TL;DR: This paper identifies a phonon-scattering-induced memory effect in superfluids, analogous to electromagnetic and gravitational memory, where a localized scattering event causes a permanent shift in the far-field velocity prepotential via a pressure pulse with nonzero first temporal moment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a laboratory analogue of electromagnetic and gravitational memory effects by identifying a similar memory phenomenon in quantum fluids—specifically, superfluid ^4He—via phonon dynamics.

Method: Analytical derivation of the memory effect using phonon scattering theory in superfluids, incorporating soft factor formalism for phonon emission and corrections from nonlinear dispersion relations.

Result: A far-field pressure pulse with vanishing time integral but nonzero first temporal moment is predicted, inducing a permanent shift in the prepotential of the velocity field; magnitude estimates for ^4He suggest experimental detectability.

Conclusion: Phonon scattering in superfluids exhibits a classical memory effect structurally analogous to those in gauge and gravitational theories, offering a controllable quantum many-body platform to probe memory phenomena.

Abstract: We identify a memory effect produced by phonon scattering in a superfluid: a localized scattering event generates a far-field pressure pulse with vanishing time integral but a nonzero first temporal moment, corresponding to a permanent shift in a prepotential for the far-field velocity. The effect is controlled by the soft factor for phonon emission, including corrections from nonlinear dispersion. In superfluid $^4$He, scattering macroscopic phonon wave packets produces a signal whose estimated magnitude may lie within experimental reach, thereby providing a laboratory analogue of electromagnetic and gravitational memory.

</details>
